    "content": "Previous Candidates:\n[\"Bipolar II Disorder\", \"Major Depressive Disorder with Psychotic Features\"]\n\nInterview Transcript:\n\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I've come here today because I've been feeling deeply depressed and sad for the past 10 months. It's just a constant, heavy weight, and I feel completely exhausted by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods of abnormally elevated or irritable mood, where you felt extremely happy, energetic, or impulsive, even if it was just for a short time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I actually had a period like that about a year ago that lasted for about a month. During that time, I felt an abnormal surge of energy and happiness, but I also became highly irritable and did some impulsive things without thinking of the consequences.\n\nDoctor: During your periods of depression or elevated mood, have you ever experienced any breaks from reality, such as hearing voices, seeing things that aren't there, or having thoughts that seem unreal or detached from reality?\n\nPatient: I don't hear voices or see things that aren't there. However, when I'm deeply depressed, I do get really unrealistic thoughts where I feel an extreme, irrational sense of guilt. I genuinely start believing that I am personally responsible for unrelated bad things happening in the world and that I deserve to be punished.\n\nDoctor: Do these unrealistic guilt thoughts occur only when you're depressed, or do they also happen during your periods of elevated mood or at other times when your mood is relatively stable?\n\nPatient: They only happen when I am in that deep, dark depression. When my mood is high, energetic, or even just normal and stable, those guilty thoughts completely disappear and don't bother me at all.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much of the past year you've spent experiencing depressive symptoms versus periods of normal or elevated mood, and have you noticed any significant gaps in time where you didn't have either depressive or manic/hypomanic symptoms?\n\nPatient: Over the past year, I've spent about 10 months feeling deeply depressed, and only about one month in that highly elevated, energetic state. That leaves just about a single month where my mood felt relatively normal and stable, so there haven't been any long gaps of feeling completely fine.\n\nDoctor: Did the unrealistic guilt thoughts you experience during depression interfere with your daily life, relationships, or work, and were they severe enough to cause significant distress or impairment?\n\nPatient: Yes, they completely took over my life and made it almost impossible to function during those ten months. I would constantly obsess over minor past mistakes and feel an overwhelming sense of responsibility for things entirely out of my control, genuinely believing I deserved to be punished. It caused me so much distress that I couldn't focus on work or connect with my family at all.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe the month when you were in the highly elevated, energetic state - did you experience any decreased need for sleep, excessive talkativeness, or involvement in activities that could have potentially painful consequences, and did others notice a change in your behavior during that time?\n\nPatient: During that month, I had overflowing energy and started doing risky things without thinking of the consequences, like spending excessive money and making rash business investments. However, I don't recall having a decreased need for sleep or being excessively talkative, and I'm really not sure if others noticed the change in my behavior.\n\nDoctor: Did your impulsive behaviors and risky decisions during the highly elevated, energetic state cause any significant problems or consequences in your personal or professional life, and did you experience any remorse or regret afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yes, my reckless spending and foolish business investments during that high-energy phase caused severe financial distress for both me and my family. While I didn't care about the consequences in the moment, I felt overwhelming guilt and regret once that high wore off and I transitioned into my depression."

The presence of depressive episodes with psychotic features (unrealistic guilt thoughts) supports Major Depressive Disorder with Psychotic Features, while the history of a hypomanic episode (elevated mood, increased energy, and impulsive behaviors) followed by a significant depressive episode suggests Bipolar II Disorder. Further questioning may be needed to differentiate between these two diagnoses based on the severity and impact of the hypomanic episode and the nature of the psychotic symptoms during depression.", "is_final": false}

    "role": "system",

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I've come here today because I've been feeling deeply depressed and sad for the past 10 months. It's just a constant, heavy weight, and I feel completely exhausted by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods of abnormally elevated or irritable mood, where you felt extremely happy, energetic, or impulsive, even if it was just for a short time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I actually had a period like that about a year ago that lasted for about a month. During that time, I felt an abnormal surge of energy and happiness, but I also became highly irritable and did some impulsive things without thinking of the consequences.\n\nDoctor: During your periods of depression or elevated mood, have you ever experienced any breaks from reality, such as hearing voices, seeing things that aren't there, or having thoughts that seem unreal or detached from reality?\n\nPatient: I don't hear voices or see things that aren't there. However, when I'm deeply depressed, I do get really unrealistic thoughts where I feel an extreme, irrational sense of guilt. I genuinely start believing that I am personally responsible for unrelated bad things happening in the world and that I deserve to be punished.\n\nDoctor: Do these unrealistic guilt thoughts occur only when you're depressed, or do they also happen during your periods of elevated mood or at other times when your mood is relatively stable?\n\nPatient: They only happen when I am in that deep, dark depression. When my mood is high, energetic, or even just normal and stable, those guilty thoughts completely disappear and don't bother me at all.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much of the past year you've spent experiencing depressive symptoms versus periods of normal or elevated mood, and have you noticed any significant gaps in time where you didn't have either depressive or manic/hypomanic symptoms?\n\nPatient: Over the past year, I've spent about 10 months feeling deeply depressed, and only about one month in that highly elevated, energetic state. That leaves just about a single month where my mood felt relatively normal and stable, so there haven't been any long gaps of feeling completely fine.\n\nDoctor: Did the unrealistic guilt thoughts you experience during depression interfere with your daily life, relationships, or work, and were they severe enough to cause significant distress or impairment?\n\nPatient: Yes, they completely took over my life and made it almost impossible to function during those ten months. I would constantly obsess over minor past mistakes and feel an overwhelming sense of responsibility for things entirely out of my control, genuinely believing I deserved to be punished. It caused me so much distress that I couldn't focus on work or connect with my family at all.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe the month when you were in the highly elevated, energetic state - did you experience any decreased need for sleep, excessive talkativeness, or involvement in activities that could have potentially painful consequences, and did others notice a change in your behavior during that time?\n\nPatient: During that month, I had overflowing energy and started doing risky things without thinking of the consequences, like spending excessive money and making rash business investments. However, I don't recall having a decreased need for sleep or being excessively talkative, and I'm really not sure if others noticed the change in my behavior.\n\nDoctor: Did your impulsive behaviors and risky decisions during the highly elevated, energetic state cause any significant problems or consequences in your personal or professional life, and did you experience any remorse or regret afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yes, my reckless spending and foolish business investments during that high-energy phase caused severe financial distress for both me and my family. While I didn't care about the consequences in the moment, I felt overwhelming guilt and regret once that high wore off and I transitioned into my depression.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if your periods of elevated mood and depressive episodes have ever occurred simultaneously, or if they have always been separate, distinct episodes, and have you experienced any periods of relatively normal mood in between these episodes?"

Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I've come here today because I've been feeling deeply depressed and sad for the past 10 months. It's just a constant, heavy weight, and I feel completely exhausted by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods of abnormally elevated or irritable mood, where you felt extremely happy, energetic, or impulsive, even if it was just for a short time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I actually had a period like that about a year ago that lasted for about a month. During that time, I felt an abnormal surge of energy and happiness, but I also became highly irritable and did some impulsive things without thinking of the consequences.\n\nDoctor: During your periods of depression or elevated mood, have you ever experienced any breaks from reality, such as hearing voices, seeing things that aren't there, or having thoughts that seem unreal or detached from reality?\n\nPatient: I don't hear voices or see things that aren't there. However, when I'm deeply depressed, I do get really unrealistic thoughts where I feel an extreme, irrational sense of guilt. I genuinely start believing that I am personally responsible for unrelated bad things happening in the world and that I deserve to be punished.\n\nDoctor: Do these unrealistic guilt thoughts occur only when you're depressed, or do they also happen during your periods of elevated mood or at other times when your mood is relatively stable?\n\nPatient: They only happen when I am in that deep, dark depression. When my mood is high, energetic, or even just normal and stable, those guilty thoughts completely disappear and don't bother me at all.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much of the past year you've spent experiencing depressive symptoms versus periods of normal or elevated mood, and have you noticed any significant gaps in time where you didn't have either depressive or manic/hypomanic symptoms?\n\nPatient: Over the past year, I've spent about 10 months feeling deeply depressed, and only about one month in that highly elevated, energetic state. That leaves just about a single month where my mood felt relatively normal and stable, so there haven't been any long gaps of feeling completely fine.\n\nDoctor: Did the unrealistic guilt thoughts you experience during depression interfere with your daily life, relationships, or work, and were they severe enough to cause significant distress or impairment?\n\nPatient: Yes, they completely took over my life and made it almost impossible to function during those ten months. I would constantly obsess over minor past mistakes and feel an overwhelming sense of responsibility for things entirely out of my control, genuinely believing I deserved to be punished. It caused me so much distress that I couldn't focus on work or connect with my family at all.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe the month when you were in the highly elevated, energetic state - did you experience any decreased need for sleep, excessive talkativeness, or involvement in activities that could have potentially painful consequences, and did others notice a change in your behavior during that time?\n\nPatient: During that month, I had overflowing energy and started doing risky things without thinking of the consequences, like spending excessive money and making rash business investments. However, I don't recall having a decreased need for sleep or being excessively talkative, and I'm really not sure if others noticed the change in my behavior.\n\nDoctor: Did your impulsive behaviors and risky decisions during the highly elevated, energetic state cause any significant problems or consequences in your personal or professional life, and did you experience any remorse or regret afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yes, my reckless spending and foolish business investments during that high-energy phase caused severe financial distress for both me and my family. While I didn't care about the consequences in the moment, I felt overwhelming guilt and regret once that high wore off and I transitioned into my depression.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if your periods of elevated mood and depressive episodes have ever occurred simultaneously, or if they have always been separate, distinct episodes, and have you experienced any periods of relatively normal mood in between these episodes?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if those high and low periods ever overlapped or if I had normal intervals in between them. I just know the high phase lasted about a month and this depression has been going on for about ten months, but the exact spacing is a bit blurry to me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned the high phase lasted about a month and the current depression has been going on for about ten months. Can you think back to the transition from the high phase to the depressive phase - was it a sudden shift, or was there a gradual change in your mood, and did you experience any mixed symptoms, such as feeling depressed and anxious at the same time?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't clearly remember how the transition between the high and low phases happened, so I can't say if it was sudden or gradual. I'm also not really sure if I experienced any mixed symptoms like anxiety during that time."

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.
